Of course Europe is also fully aware of what we are doing and are in regular communication with DG HOME, which also has responsibility and financial instruments to support this. I do not think the problem will be money. And sometimes, you know, Europe is hiding behind the money, so that it can actually get us into the problem and not take the hardest political decisions that we have been talking about. The distribution has been done in the best possible way - I repeat - always in consultation with the local communities and always making specific forecasts for the islands, which should know what their future is in this situation, but also for the local communities, for the municipalities, where we will create larger structures than the ones that exist today. We agree that there must be closed pre-regulatory structures on the islands. I don't think anyone's questioning it. I don't know what SYRIZA says about this. I imagine SYRIZA disagrees. However, I would like to remind you, Mrs Gennimata, since the debate brought this to light, that on 4 March 2016 - I remember it, because it was my birthday day and I had just taken over the President of the New Democracy - we had held a meeting in the Presidential Palace on the issue of refugees - immigration. The agreement at that time and on the part of the government at that time was that we are going to go in this direction. You remember that well. No, of course, absolutely nothing. The main axes, therefore, that had been in place since then, have not been implemented by the previous government. They'll be applied by her. And, of course, you have every right - and you will do well - to be here, to criticise us and to check us for the timetable, which we have set, which, yes, is ambitious, but it is a timetable that we will do what we are going through, so that we can implement it. The fourth point, to which we agree, is that we must guard our borders differently and more effectively. We told you what we're doing to Evros, what we're doing at sea. We told you how we want to strengthen the port and know that the maximum possible redistribution of forces in the port has already been achieved so that now that the tourist season is over we can transport ships from the rest of Greece to the eastern Aegean, we can get the best possible presence. The issue has also been raised in NATO, because this NATO mission, which exists, and it has its usefulness, because on a ship there is a Greek officer and a Turkish officer and that is useful so that no one can claim that there is no communication between the two parties. We are therefore sending a clear message that our borders are protected in a different way: land and sea. I repeat, however, that this will ultimately be Europe's responsibility. We are positive that Europe should be able to support us on this initiative, but, as I have said before, it is not just the mood, it is not enough, it is not enough, as the vessels of FRONTEX are, but we need to know exactly what is going on at sea and that is not at this moment, at least in Europe, quite clearly. I close, Mrs Gennamas, by telling you once again that this problem is an important problem, a problem that is lasting, a problem for which Greek society understands that there are no magical solutions. Other problems can be solved very quickly, but this is extremely complex. It is a problem, which has a strong human dimension, and this human dimension, which relates primarily to the most vulnerable, the most vulnerable groups, there I believe we cannot and have no right to make a discount. As emergency vaccines were sent to Samos and Lesvos to vaccinate children outside this protection framework, the same protection will always be given to the most vulnerable. And most vulnerable, of course, are the unaccompanied minors. We have about a thousand children right now in the organized structures, but we have four thousand children, who are either in Moria or in other places in extremely problematic conditions, or are in the streets of Athens, unfortunately with tragic stories, which you all know about. I have therefore made a personal commitment, and there will be an immediate announcement of a national coordinator on this issue, to deal with it. I have to say to you - and I have to close with this - that it is not a good sign of European solidarity that we have specifically tried for unaccompanied minors to get in touch with all European countries and say: for God's sake, for three thousand children we are talking about! Will we keep the thousands of five hundred, the remaining three thousand children cannot be allocated to twenty-seven countries so that Europe can show solidarity in the most sensitive sector and in numbers which are ultimately insignificant for large European countries? Unfortunately, the answer - and I am sorry to say it - does not honor Europe. The answer we have received was not positive. However, we, you and we all will continue to insist on putting the matter in a serious position. We assume our responsibilities for what we have to do in the country. This makes it easier for us in our discussions with Europe. All that has been done, the change in the legislative framework, the greater emphasis we place on border guarding, are weapons that we have in our negotiation. So I will tell you that your criticism is reasoned and your tone is the right one. I would simply ask you to bear in mind that we have no reason to make it easier either in Europe or in Greece, so that we do not present the situation as it is. We have no reason to present it worse than it is. I do not believe that it is your intentions, and I say this quite clearly, and I do not see this from the tone of your question, but ultimately it is a problem that concerns us all and we will all solve it together. Thank you, everyone.

In our opinion, it serves the eternal effort to restore the urban political system, which ensures that it is "makered" often densely so that it hides its true anti-popular face. This time, the political "lifting" attempted by the Constitutional Review, focuses mainly on changing the constitutional provision on responsibility for Ministers, the known Article 86, which can be improved, but remains its essence. We are clear here about the position of the Communist Party of Greece on this article. The position of the JRC is the complete abolition of this article, because it does not have any work but it does not have any work to be done by the House to be a pre-reviewing committee, to be a court, to regulate all these issues, which must finally be taken away from Parliament at some point and the Ministers must be controlled and accountable for justice, just as every Greek citizen and, if you want, even more stringent than the ordinary citizen. Because, you know, the mere worker, the farmer, the professional chasing the flea-frame and having a minister who handles very serious issues, has immunity and cannot justice refer him to the court, if necessary, because he has special criminal treatment. Because it is specific criminal treatment, ladies and gentlemen, of the Ministers, that a preliminary examination should be carried out by the House and not by the Prosecutor, such as for all other Greek citizens. It is special criminal treatment that this preliminary examination of the Ministers should be carried out by a committee composed of MPs, and for ordinary citizens it is carried out by a court with the assistance of the police. It is a special penal treatment for the ministers that the ministers are prosecuted by a decision of the House and not by the prosecutor, as is the case for ordinary citizens. And, of course, it is special penal treatment that the House can, by its decision, withdraw or suspend the prosecution of Ministers, which is not the case for ordinary citizens, and, of course, for cases relating to criminal liability of Ministers not to be dealt with by the ordinary criminal courts, depending on the gravity of the act, as is the case with ordinary citizens, but to be brought by the special court. This is all a special, special criminal treatment of the ministers. And all this with the proposed Review remains, both responsible for SYRIZA and the New Democracy. Eventually, that's why it's not enough to remove the depreciation deadline. In spite of the fact that this depreciation deadline is being abolished, that is to say, the matter of limitation - and we welcome it being abolished with both hands - the substance of the provision on special treatment, as we have said, is, unfortunately, still a criminal treatment of the ministers. The New Democracy and SYRIZA's justification for this choice is interesting here. The New Democracy from this step forward claimed that this, the special criminal treatment of the ministers, is imposed by the seriousness of ministerial duties. You know, however, this directly contradicts the reason that it has led to this review and it could well work and act more strongly as an argument for the abolition of Article 86, which is completely complete. SYRIZA is now trying to hide his complete turn on the subject. Previously, four and five years ago, SYRIZA spoke about the total abolition of Article 86 and now merely adds an interpretative statement on the non-deleting of ministerial offences, which, SYRIZA says, presupposes the existence of the relevant article, so why not abolish it. We think that this legal argument is unscathed. A transitional provision with the same content of the interpretative declaration also ensures that Article 86 is abolished and that there is no limitation. But SYRIZA's political argument is even more revealing. From where SYRIZA asked for the abolition of Article 86 a few years ago now, he is calling for this article to remain, because it is, he says, a filter of protection for the Ministers against pressure and interference in the legitimate exercise of their duties. Now, because one needs protection for the legitimate exercise of his duties, this has not been clarified. Let's go a little bit on today's debate, and I'm starting with Article 96. Although we have no illusions - we do not belong to this club - about the mission and the role of justice in the urban state, we nonetheless consider that there is room for modernisation and improvements in the functioning and administration of justice. That is why our party, when the Constitutional Review began and tabled its proposals at that time, included those proposals and those relating to justice. The culmination of these proposals is that the appointment of the Head of Justice by the respective government, which - here Is heard - of the Presidents and Vice-Presidents of the three Supreme Courts, the Prosecutor of the Ice Bay, the General Commissioner of the Audit Conference and the General Commissioner of the Administrative Courts, should finally be stopped. Of course, among our proposals it was that the leadership of justice should in any case be elected by a broader elite. We have also tabled the proposal in this constitutional revision to abolish the relevant constitutional provision - unacceptable, in our opinion - which provides for intervention, that is, the Minister of Justice in question, both in the promotions of judges and in their possible disciplinary actions. At some point all this must be stopped and abolished. Of course, all of this has been tacitly rejected by both the New Democracy and SYRIZA, simply revealing their disobedient hypocrisy when each other is accused of intervening on either side in justice. However, there is something else, you know, and this is not only the small end-of-life value of words of independence of justice, but also the other great things that are often heard here in the House about the differences of powers since, according to the Constitution itself, these three powers are ultimately not divided between them and with Chinese walls. Finally, we called for the abolition of military justice and the arrest of the military offences by the ordinary criminal courts. We do not understand and certainly do not agree with the desirability of the existence and functioning of the barracks, the tactics and the emergency services, both in the present and in any circumstances. Besides, in popular conscience, the militia, you know, wake up very bad memories, since they are connected to the darkest pages of Greek history, the most brutal persecutions and mass executions of popular fighters. We shall now go to Rule 101, which refers to the so-called independent authorities. Here too, the proposals from the New Democracy and SYRIZA are moving in the same direction to strengthen the role of these independent authorities, restore their prestige and, in short, upgrade overall. And that, you know, is not a symptom at all. It is because of the nature and role of these so-called independent authorities, that is to say, acting as instruments for legitimising the power and the interests of capital. That is precisely why they are being adopted and operated. In the end, however much as these independent authorities, supposedly, are modernising, however much as they attempt to do with SYRIZA's proposals, especially those independent authorities, to appear as accountants in society, ultimately the mission and role of these authorities is determined by the framework within which they are established and operated. And this framework is beyond the limits of the sovereign civil state policy. That is why these so-called independent administrative authorities, both the five constitutionally guaranteed and the others, which are constantly multiplying, are only temptation to implement the dominant policy and control mechanisms, but not from the point of view of the people's interests, but from the point of view of the capital's interests, as wheelmen and arbitrators of conflicting business interests. It is no accident and we need to remind ourselves in which areas the operation of such independent administrative authorities has started. They first appeared in those sectors of finance where the markets were released from the state's bonds, as they were saying, were privatised, namely, in the field of telecommunications, energy, ports, just to play the role of regulator and the role of arbitrator in sharing the "piet" of the profits of privatizations that started in these sectors since then. As for the other independent administrative authorities that are now growing like mushrooms and which are also relevant in critical areas of social life, it is worth seeing certain stigmas. Let's start with the Privacy Authority. With the recent bill passed last September, which was passed both by New Democracy and by SYRIZA, the institutional framework for the Personal Data Protection Authority was redefined. The "Protection" within many introductory forms. Do you know why? What happened to this frame? What does this framework provide for? Neither too little nor much, according to the law anymore, allows the processing of personal data not only by state but by private bodies, by opening up a dangerous road that can lead to a new system of more up-graded enclaves by collecting and exchanging data on politics, say, opinions, trade union activities and organisation and so much so annoying about the system and the power of capital. This is the legal framework that the Independent Privacy Authority will supervise and verify its application. Let us go to another committee which has a direct relationship with the workers. Fifteen months are unpaid those workers at Rio's casino in Patras. The employer owes them 15 months' time, despite the fact that there are parallel judicial decisions which justify these workers. So what did these workers do? Last May they appealed to such an independent administrative authority in the Committee on the Supervision and Control of Games. Indeed, according to the law, this committee would have to make a decision within sixty days in two months. This decision should have been made by the end of July at the latest. November we have and the decision has not yet been taken by this independent authority. What should these workers and us and everyone think about the independence of this principle? Finally, I should like to refer to the so-called Competition Committee, which was indeed very long-standing in the previous period, that is to say, the committee which takes care of the competitiveness of business groups. Has this Competition Committee ever come to an end to the spread of unemployment and poverty? Has the workers in our country been prevented from impoverishing, all that the worker lives today, but also the workers of all the countries of the European Union? No, of course not. After all, this competitiveness serves, and for this competitiveness, labour rights are being smashed. Does it have the name 'Competition Committee'? They do not, however, have any connection with the concerns, the concerns of the people, but they are firmly serving the designs and strategy of the system. What is, let's say - by closing - the Independent Public Income Authority despite the firm and unscathed hand of the state tax evacuation mechanism that manages and implements the tax raid against the people's strata? As a party, we are arguing with these committees, and we are calling for the abolition of the specific article which prescribes these so-called independent administrative authorities. I would like to say a few words about Article 102 on local government. It's recommended that local folk assemblies and local referendums should be introduced, so I can start from here. However, objectively, you know that all of this within the framework of the sovereign policy - whose direction does not change despite the rotation of governments, because of the general strategy of the European Union - by exploiting also the characteristics of the proximity and subsidiarity of local self-government, the terminologies being passed directly from the European Union, can only operate - local people's assemblies, local referendums - as substitutes for popular participation, as levers of integration and popular subordination. And, of course, little - to nothing at all - they have a movement of initiatives from the bottom, such as people, to say, committees which, through their racing activities, have helped, among other things, many peoples towards the auctions or the reconnection of their electricity. With regard to local referendums, we think that the same applies to national referendums. So, who's the one asking the question? How does the question arise? These are key issues. In what circumstances will these referendums take place? Their effect is not binding. I'm talking about locals. Are you going to tell me about the nationals? We all saw and lived what happened. Why shouldn't it be binding? Here, you know, a typical example from Thessaloniki in the 2014 referendum on privatization of HSY, an informal referendum, but with very large popular participation, the result of which the sovereign policy finally managed to assimilate it, since the question put to the people of the city at the time was not affected, the privatization of the city's water supply was not affected. And the womb was - and it is, of course - the policy of liberating the European Union and water, as in energy and in a number of other sectors of the economy, of course. However, when the roots of the problem are not affected by a referendum, as was the case with the referendum on the EBRD, the sovereign policy can be well integrated, assimilated and sometimes even exploited, in order to pass its policy, as was done by SYRIZA in the case of EBRD, which proceeded with privatization. Finally, the JRC's position on local administration is well known. Our position opposes the current situation, in which the transfer of powers of the so-called "decentralisation" is accompanied by the economic strangulation of the municipalities, causing the local administration to become a further tax-driven mechanism, a lever for strengthening the reciprocity and generalisation of social services, so that as their main source the resources of local government or administration can have the reciprocity and entrepreneurship, while state funding can only have a symbolic balance. It is a typical argument, which is relevant to what we have said and relates to the so-called 'new revenue tools', as those of the municipalities are called. We are, of course, talking here about the euphemism of the real estate of the municipalities, which is nothing but a general raid, literally, a general attack by the so-called 'investors' on municipal property, its resources and its infrastructure. We do not fail to mention what happened a few days ago with the Prime Minister, who recently told the regional authorities that state funding, he says, will depend on whether the central political line is actually being implemented. In conclusion, on the occasion of the debate on the current unity in the Review Committee, which is the latter, we want once again to reaffirm the JRC's firm position on the constitutionalisation of the state's obligation to ensure all the necessary financial resources in the municipalities and regions with funding from the state budget. Thank you, Mr President, and your tolerance.

The DENMARK will also meet on a regular basis with specific agendas. The representatives of the elected representatives shall put forward proposals and other members shall put questions and counter-proposals. At the end of the Conference, the IPCC shall act by a majority and submit to the region a recommendation. The region, if consensual, acts. If the EPC does not agree, and the EPC shall return with the same recommendation after two years, the recommendation shall enter a negative roll-call vote on the regional council, requiring an enhanced majority to be rejected. In other words, regional authorities and representatives should specifically explain why they are responding to and not disciplining the request of the IPPCs. As you can see, the issue of local assemblies and initiatives is going one step further. Last thing I left the independent authorities and their leadership. At the same time, it demonstrates the government's willingness to set up a prime-priority centralism, which ultimately alters the form of the culture itself. European law itself, in Directive 1 of 2019, states about these principles. National administrative competition authorities shall be protected against external interference or political pressures which may jeopardise their independent assessment of the issues they are being addressed. And then the directive becomes even more specific. The leadership of such a authority may be terminated only if it no longer fulfils the conditions for carrying out the tasks or if it is found guilty of serious infringement of a duty under national law. So to dismantle any suspicions that the independent authorities and the political forces are either hugging or trenches, we are proposing the choice of a dilemma of the heads of independent authorities, a body that I will not analyse, because I have already referred to this in detail. In order to stimulate independence and transparency, we also propose that the plenary sessions of the independent authorities be public, very clearly with some necessary exceptions. ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-22-S1-commons.u139	Before we move to the special rapporteurs, I will give the floor to the New Democracy Parliamentary Representative, Mr Stylianides. Mr. Comrade, you have the floor.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-22-S1-commons.u140	Thank you, Mr President. Thank you very much, Mr President. Before I enter the subject of today's, last theme of unity, I would like to say, because I have previously heard Mr Katrugalos, that the violation of duty, referred to in Article 259 of the Criminal Code, is an offence other than the bribery referred to in Article 235 of the Criminal Code, because the first, that is to say Article 259, relates to the duties of officials and therefore the Minister, when he violates them. That's what he said. But according to Article 235, which is the bribe, the objective nature of this crime needs to be taken unfairly by the official and therefore by the Minister for Energy or Failure to act in the performance of his duties, i.e. in the exercise of the duty of the Minister. This is an answer to comment. I am now entering the main section, ladies and gentlemen. The revision of Article 96(5) shall be necessary in order to enable military judges to be enclosed with the necessary guarantees of operational and personal independence, such as those provided for in Article 87(1) of the Constitution. That is why, in our proposal, explicit reference is made to the equation of military and political judges as follows: Members of the Judiciary of the Armed Forces are surrounded by lean guarantees of functional and personal independence of other regular judges under Article 87(1) of the Constitution. The provision of a law on the graduation of the judiciary officers of the Armed Forces with other regular judicial officers is also added, the composition of the Supreme Judiciary of this body, the disciplinary boards and, of course, the inspection committees. The law already adopted is Law 2304/1945 of the Armed Forces Judiciary Code and Law 2287/1995 of the Military Criminal Code. The need for a stronger clarification of the independent and impartial nature of the judiciary is based on Article 6(1) of the European Convention on Human Rights, which, of course, concerns not only trials for citizens, but also citizens of military status. This view strengthens a number of decisions by the European Court of Human Rights, particularly against Turkey. They are the Ciraklur, Ynkul and Otsalan cases. In all cases, it is argued that they cannot enjoy personal and operational independence in the performance of their duties as judges when, due to their parallel status as officers, they are dependent on bodies of the executive, i.e. in this case the military hierarchy. This is what comes to protect the provision of Article 96(5), which defines the grading equivalence of functions, the composition of the relevant councils and the equation with the other ordinary judges, because independence and impartiality, as the European Court of Human Rights maintains, must not just be guaranteed by their organisation and function, but also be seen and, at the same time, demonstrated. Furthermore, the recommendation of the Committee of Ministers of the Council of Europe on the protection of human rights of members of the Armed Forces, adopted on 24 February 2010, expressly provides in paragraph 34 that ‘the organisation and functioning of the military courts, where they exist, should fully ensure the right of everyone to an appropriate, independent and impartial court at every stage of legal proceedings’. The need for this new proposal for revision and supplementing Article 96(5) emerged, among other things, from Decision 2857/2003/EC of the Council of State, which considered whether the State could establish for judiciary officers the wages of other judiciary officers or the wages of the military. The judgment then held that, in the case of the military judges, Article 88(2) of the Constitution, concerning the remuneration of judges, cannot apply in this case, otherwise the existing Article 96(5)(a). Such interpretations have taken place, of course, in other decisions of the Council of State, as well as in judgments of court proceedings, in matters relating to the personal and service situation of the officials of military justice. However, this has to be cured, because, as in practice, the case law, the remuneration of the judicial officers, whoever they are, whether military or political, is an element of personal and operational independence. The equation, which is reflected in the new provision, therefore, interprets this ambiguity that leads the Council of State to the above interpretation of wages and confirms that this independence and institutional equality with other judges are not for the sake of their industry, but for the sake of citizens who are not allowed under the Constitution, because they are military, to be tried by judges with limited guarantees of independence. I'm going to the matter of the independent authorities. The independent authorities appeared to be a phenomenon in the United States of America and were transported some delay to Europe. It could be argued that they constitute the institutional confession of the political system's failure to manage in itself the necessity of a new horizontal, this time, a distinction of powers, a distinction between the market, technology and politics. If each of these areas is allowed to act alone and uncontrollably, it tends to impose on others. If, for example, the policy is allowed to control the market, we will lead to a state-reporting model, corresponding to what has failed in the Soviet Union, with an attack on too many cases, even on individual freedoms. If, on the other hand, technocracy is allowed to control politics, then we risk leading to an inappropriate rationale, which is often unable to perceive the strengths or wishes of society, by transforming democracy into a status of experts, that is, what the German theory calls 'Fachidioten', that is,'state of skilled idiots'. If, finally, the market is to control policy, we are at risk of leading to an absolute conflict, where the strength of specific interests will multiply greedy, by ordering bills at the expense of society as a whole and, possibly, at the expense of democracy itself. The independent authorities come to impose this very new horizontal separation of powers, defining dynamically, but accurately the complementary and inter-control role of the three different sites: the market area, the area of technology and the policy area. So gradually, with their operation and their decisions, it is clear that: Firstly, the market must be the one that produces wealth, creates jobs, despise the concept of "driving" and "interesting" as the driving creative forces of man, provided that the individual passes through public, social and national interests and obeys rules. Secondly, technocracy can contribute to other areas with fresh knowledge and modern information, but without, of course, replacing them in their operation. Finally, it is the policy that lays down the rules, supervises their implementation and defines a common national and social vision, namely a strategy, in which, of course, it will fit the small dream of each family, every business, every citizen and, ultimately, every human being. For these reasons, I believe that newly emerging independent authorities can be typologically distinguished into four major categories: The first includes those that act as institutional guarantees for the effective protection of certain individual rights, such as the Privacy Authority in Article 9A of the Constitution and the Communications Security Authority in Article 19(2) of the Constitution. The second category includes principles which exercise supervision in large areas or in the entire public administration, in particular as regards the right to petition, as specified in Article 10 of the Constitution or the appointment of civil servants in Article 103 of the Constitution. These are no more than the AACC in Article 103 par. 7 and the Citizens' Counsel at Article 103, par. No 9 of the Constitution. The Consumers' Counselor does not belong to the constitutionally protected authorities, but it monitors the rules. The third category includes independent bodies which exercise powers mainly relating to the functioning of the market, as a whole or in specific areas, such as the Competition Committee, the National Telecommunications and Post Commission, the Energy Regulatory Authority, etc. These are not provided for by the Constitution, but by law and are affected, not only by the national legislator, but also by European law. They refer mainly to the state's economic function and economic freedom. I would like to say here that we could actually have the 'form' of the state, so that the way these authorities operate is in line with the rules of operation of the constitutional authorities. And we don't, of course, put them in the Constitution, but at least there's this correspondence in the operation. This also responds in a way to the Public Procurement Authority, which had also tabled a letter to the committee on this subject, so that it can see whether it is available to prescribe it. Fourthly, a critical mixed category of authorities, combining the characteristics of the three previous categories, is the one which includes the National Broadcasting Council in Article 15(1). On the one hand, it guarantees and supervises and ensures that the Constitution, which has decisive powers on the one hand. These independent authorities are useful when they guarantee rights and there they must in any case be constitutionally guaranteed, and when they seek more transparency in the functioning of the administration this must be reflected in relevant arrangements in the Code of Administrative Procedure. On the other hand, when it is a manifestation of the political system's inability to assume its responsibilities and try to transfer them to the shoulders of non-political persons, the establishment of this function must be vain. Independent authorities cannot be regarded as institutional counterweights operating against the principle of controls and balances of our parliamentary system. On the other hand, they must act as guarantees for a legal, democratic and pluralist city, by protecting the autonomy of politics against overconcentration of economic, communication and, ultimately, political influence. As in the traditional distinction between legislative, judicial and executive powers, the judicial control of anti-constitutionalism, parliamentary control, the dissolution of the House, the necessary adoption of acts, for example, by the President of the United States from the Senate, etc., must also be applied as the independent authorities, not as a counterweight to the parliamentary character, but as a counterweight to the relationship between the market, technology and politics, in the context of a new, as I said before, a horizontal distinction of powers. The relationship between the authorities and Parliament is governed by Rule 101a itself in paragraph 3, which, in addition to parliamentary scrutiny, refers to the Rules of Procedure of Parliament. Inspection is caused by the Minister or by direct hearings in Articles 66 et seq. of the Constitution and 138 A and 43A(2) of the Rules of Procedure of the Parliament. This, and the special relationship of the independent authorities with the House, obliges us to revise this article, making it easier to serve them, but without losing their supra-party and independent character and without creating an institutional vacuum at the same time as the inability to form them. To date, the practice of seeking unanimity and, nonetheless, a majority of 4/5 of the Conference of Presidents has led to a difficulty or even a failure to form the authorities. This was addressed by Law 3051/2002, as amended by Law 4055/2012, in order to automatically extend the term of office of the members of the independent authority to six months if there is a problem of new establishment. The extension of the period of six months shall also apply if some members disappear or leave, but without prejudice to the possibility of forming a quorum. The Council of State in its entirety, by Decision 3515/2013 and later on 2583/2017, has allowed the authorities to continue to operate until new members have become members, but only for a reasonable time to be judged in the circumstances. The proposal of the New Democracy has to deal with the problem that has arisen in practice, replacing the unanimity and the enhanced majority of 4/5 with the 3/5 of the Conference of Presidents. This facilitates the replacement process or institutionalises the extension and thus ensures the continuous, undisturbed functioning of the independent authorities. And that is a guarantee even for our citizens. At the same time, the prestige of the principle is protected from micro-party purposes, which may prevent the replacement of members. And, of course, the parliamentary accountability of the authorities and their parliamentary control guarantee their complementary role, facilitates Parliament in its work, strengthens their democratic legitimacy and strengthens their effectiveness through the obligation of parliamentary accountability, which is usually done in the form of reports. In conclusion, ladies and gentlemen, because I am actually ending my speech during the procedure of the Revision House, let me, as chairman of the committee, thank the Bureau and the Members of this House for their complete and excellent support in the difficult work we have undertaken, all the rapporteurs and special speakers, my colleagues in the Bureau and Parliamentary Representatives of all the parties, the political parties themselves, the political groups and all my fellow Members for their contribution to a dialogue of the highest political and scientific level, in a very serious effort at national level. The different ideological origins have not prevented us from reliving a initially deceased and mutilated review process. Starting from the hundred and fifty-four points of disagreement in the proposed House and the forty-nine points of disagreement in the Revision Committee, we have managed to meet on nine important points of agreement - probably Monday - and to vote on nine major changes, namely, much more and certainly much more important than other reform efforts, such as the 1986 and 2008 one. Our decisions: Firstly, they support the Greek family with the minimum guaranteed income in Article 21(1). Secondly, they shall ensure governmental and political stability by preventing the highest rule from being elected under Article 32(4). Thirdly, they upgrade Parliament by facilitating the setting up of a committee of inquiry and by the minor opposition, in accordance with Article 68. Fourthly, they restore the position of the Member with the strictness of asylum in Article 62. Fifthly, they shield the credibility of policy with the equality of minister and ordinary citizen in matters of limitation in the criminal proceedings, in accordance with Article 86(3). Sixth, strengthen the independence of military justice in accordance with Article 96(5). Seventhly, it facilitates horizontal separation of powers between the market, technology and policy, by facilitating the emergence of independent authorities in Article 101A(2) of the Constitution. Eighth, they shall ensure the activation of citizens by establishing the popular legislative initiative in Article 76(6) of the Constitution. Ninth, they grow Greece, facilitating the vote of the universal Hellenism and making the national Parliament an expressioner not only of the people but also of the nation. For these reasons, ladies and gentlemen, I finally believe that this Review will leave behind the indelible quality seal and thank you very much for your contribution to all its phases.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-22-S1-commons.u143	Next speaker, the special rapporteur from SYRIZA, Mr Theophilos Xanthiopoulos.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-22-S1-commons.u144	Thank you very much, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, in my report I shall be taking both issues, and Articles 96(5) and 101 concerning the independent authorities. And I will be in my time, because I understand that we have colleagues from yesterday who have been afflicted. Ladies and gentlemen, military justice, in the form we know today, is seen as a conquest of the ideological outrage brought to the world by the French Revolution, even though the award of military justice was exclusively a matter of the supreme commander to the commander. The French model of military justice was followed by most of the member states of the international community with smaller variations. Our country has not been an exception to this practice since 1822, during the French Revolution, the introduction of the relevant French military criminal rules by means of a resolution from the First National Assembly of Epidaurus. In our country, alongside the first attempts to establish regular troops according to the pattern of the armies of the European countries, in particular by modelling the organization of the French troops, the total introduction of French military law, as I said, was adopted in 1822, which, in view of the need for'military flexibility' to prosper the troops and 'inhibit what is from the unexplained conflict', provided for in its 9-1-22 resolution, on the one hand, that'military crimes are judged by a military council appointed by the administration and the condemnation decision is taken after the ratification of the administration' and, on the other, that 'the military Codex of France, with the necessary prerogatives, which has to be exposed to the custody of the administration, also applies to the military of Greece'. It is, essentially, the introduction and primaryity of military justice in Greece. After the release of our country from the Turkish yoke and for the restoration, establishment and operation of the independent Greek state, then the regular troops of Greece, the then Governor of Greece, Ioannis Kapodistrias, began to organise, because he thought it was not easy to draw up a military penal code, originally published the Organisation of the Thousands, setting the basis for an incomplete, even military criminal law, by providing for military courts. It then adopted its resolution of 21-10-28, which established the practice to date, the military and naval criminal laws in force in France. All the above efforts do not seem to have had a substantial effect, since, in fact, in France, the text of the current Military Criminal Code had been finalised, due to the successive reform efforts, with the result that the administration of justice, at least to the Greek troops of the time, was at the discretion of the commander concerned. Finally, in 1860, after being passed by the Parliament and the Senate, the first complete Military Criminal Code of our country, which was a imitation and, in many respects, an accurate translation of the French Military Criminal Code of 1857, which generally referred to the first, but remained in force as regards the main naval offences, the earlier 'due to offences and penalties' laws introduced by Captain Ioannis Kapodistrias. I would only mention that the laws of a qualitative, legislative nature were intended for the voluntary armies of their time. That is why, over time, they ceased to respond to the needs of the National Army, both in terms of suppression of criminal acts, which undermined its security and discipline, as well as in terms of guarantees given to the soldiers. In 1936, therefore, there was a new attempt to create a Military Criminal Code, which was not completed, while in 1939, the Ministry of Justice finally set up a five-member committee to draw up a draft Military Criminal Code, which was submitted in January 1941, but which was prevented by the Second World War and the case remained incomplete. The resolution of the 1974 Constitution is undoubtedly considered to have led to developments towards the reform of Greek military justice, since such provisions were included in Section IV of the Constitution, entitled "Organisation and jurisdiction of the courts", which contains arrangements for the criminal courts of the country. And it was predicted that “the regarding the functioning of the military courts, naval courts and courts, to which individuals cannot be subject, are regulated by special laws”, Article 96(4)(a) of the Constitution. Article 96(5) below provided for the officials of the military courts, the same fundamental guarantees of functional and personal independence as those surrounding the common criminal and political courts. In the long period between the French Revolution and today, the tendency to "wean" military justice by the military in itself is reinforced, precisely because military justice is judging offences only by soldiers, without being different from the administration of justice to the rest of society. After all, the establishment of a School of Military Law serves this need and therefore professional military judges do not have a seat at the court's headquarters, as the grounds for rescinding them as judges have turned out. Today, when the socio-political causes of the institution of military justice have changed irresponsiblely in this area of justice, they have also been influenced, in turn, by the predominant view that, as a judiciary, it must only be granted by judges, the time has come to amend Article 96(5) of the Constitution, so that military courts are made up of members of the Armed Forces Judicial Corps, for which members have to provide the same guarantees of personal and operational independence as colleagues of the other courts. This regulation is a mature demand of society and also of the judiciary itself, so that today the review process can synchronise its pace with the existing social need. The proposed regulation reflects the dialectic course of demilitarisation of military justice, in order to become justice awarded by judges to civilians who have the status of military. Our proposal to amend the relevant article is in this direction, namely, that of all constitutional guarantees of the personal and operational independence of military justice, including the provisions on remuneration. After all, the term "military judge" does not express the military status of the judge but his functional role. This is therefore a proposal that enhances the general functioning of the judiciary, cures a chronic injustice, but also has a further important dimension. Neither symbolically nor essentially does it mean in a democratic European state to maintain any discrimination in the constitutional guarantees provided by judges. In this direction, we believe that the proposed amendment enhances the quality and validity of the judicial function as a whole, as it removes the existing inequalities. With regard to 110A and the institutional role of the independent authorities in the Greek legal order - so that I do not abuse time and take the vital time of colleagues who are waiting - I would like to say that, as long ago as yesterday, it is actually a way of agreeing to help with the functioning of the public administration where it cares. I would like to say that, in essence, we must not have much and inflation of such principles. That is why we have set the prerequisites for the Parliament's decision to establish them. [[At this point the end of the speaking time of the honourable Member is ringing at the end of the bell.]] And so that I do not exceed my time, Mr President, just to say that we are making a regulation, in which we say that the independent authorities must be decided by a qualified majority. Furthermore, their administration should be determined by the decision of 4/5. Since, within eight months of the end of the term of office of the outgoing members of the independent authority, a quorum of 4/5 has not been achieved and within 30 days - I add to the excellence of the legislative wording - we have to go to the 3/5 majority. With these proposals I conclude my introduction. I would like to thank all my fellow Members, the Bureau and the rapporteurs for an excellent procedure, both on the debate in the Committee on Revision of the Constitution and on the House itself. Thank you very much.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-22-S1-commons.u145	The special rapporteur from New Democracy, Mr Zetta Makreis, has the floor.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-22-S1-commons.u146	Thank you, Mr President. Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, all of us who have had the honour of participating in the Constitutional Review Committee, many times in our contributions and in our positions, have reverted and effectively appreciated the previous three revisions from 1975 to today. And we did so by assessing the trends of the times during which these were carried out, the accumulated experience gained, but also the acquired experiences that were acquired at intervals of time. But, ladies and gentlemen, we must be honest with Greek society. First of all, this review is carried out in an internationally more constitutionalised environment. The National Constitution itself is international, as it is under the influence of the law of the European Union, of international law and, in particular, of the European Convention on Human Rights. All the Member States of the European Union now follow the current trend, the phenomenon that is scientifically called'multi-level constitutionalism', i.e., because of the influence of the above international legal rules, is related to the value of the national constitution. [[This is where the President-in-Office takes over Mr. KONSTANTINOPOULOS.]] However, its importance remains extremely important in matters relating to national constitutional identity, which they - and they must protect - all the Member States and even the Member States of the European Union, because it is precisely linked to national sovereignty. The constitutional review is therefore very important and is not often done. Nevertheless, over time, from one Review to the other, many informal constitutional revisions can be carried out through judicial control of constitutionality and the interpretation of courts, European and international law or through the European Convention on Human Rights. Somehow, therefore, informally constitutionally, the independent authorities made their appearance in the Greek legal order in the 1980s, initially as independent administrative authorities, while some of them were then guaranteed by the Constitution itself by the 2001 Review. In the constitutional text, the term 'administrative' has been deleted in order to give greater emphasis to the term 'independence'. With Article 101a of the Constitution, in conjunction with the specific provisions relating to each authority, the basic institutional features, as well as issues concerning the terms of office of the designated members of the Boards, the staff and the qualifications of the members of the independent authorities are regulated. The apparent need for the incorporation of the independent authorities into the Greek legal order is primarily based on the effective protection of individual rights, which, because of the rapid technological development, had to be dressed with additional institutional guarantees, precisely because there are new types of threats, such as telecommunications or privacy of private life. There should therefore be additional institutional guarantees to prevent and control these threats in the areas I have just mentioned. The other need is to cover weaknesses and shortcomings in political power linked to the crisis in modern representative parliamentary democracy. The creation of the independent authorities is the result of the need for the political bodies of the states themselves to be politically neutralised in very serious areas of state power, areas for which they are embarrassed in managing them or inability to protect and apply the sovereignty and neutrality of the public administration, for issues relating to the functioning of very critical sectors of the market. Independent authorities are therefore a form of strengthening and shielding the legal phenomenon. Also evident are the technical reasons why we are leading to more and more formations of independent authorities. The impartial formation of executives, the highest level in terms of qualifications, the competitive advantage vis-à-vis others to adopt acts with full justification, is some of them. Finally, with regard to their typology, the authorities were initially classified into four major categories, later for reasons of briefness and practicability, together with two: constitutionally guaranteed and non-constitutionally guaranteed. The first original category of four comprises independent authorities operating as institutional guarantees for constitutional rights. It is the Privacy Authority, the Privacy Authority. The second major category includes the authorities that supervise public administration, such as the Citizens' Counselor and the Higher Staff Selection Board, which is not named within the Constitution, but directly refers to the revised Article 103(7) of the Constitution. The third major category is the independent market surveillance authorities, which are not constitutionally regulated, such as the Competition Committee, the Energy Regulatory Authority, the National Telecommunications and Post Committee, but are certainly completely tolerated and desirable. Finally, there is the category that includes the mixed nature of independent authorities, which combines the characteristics of all three previous categories, such as the National Broadcasting Council. The first question we have to answer is whether there will be any regulation on constitutionally established principles, since the Constitution only safeguards the four that I have mentioned. The answer is obvious: They are covered by both Article 56(3) of the Constitution and by the case-law of the Council of State. With regard to the appointment of members, this issue is of paramount importance, as it has troubled our public life quite a bit in recent years. A characteristic example is the delay in the renewal process of the Board of Directors. the PSE, as well as other authorities. I would remind you that even mistrust was expressed by the previous government in the Conference of Presidents as to whether it is possible for the latter to choose meritly and validly, with an increased majority of 4/5, the persons administering the independent authorities. We have reached the point of S.H.E.'s decisions. to annul the decisions of the independent authorities on grounds of non-legal composition, as the term of office of the members expired. This quasi-minority is not at all pleasant, nor is it institutionally insurmountable. Therefore, in addition to the subsidiary assistance of Law 3051/2002 and the most recent Law 4055/2012, to extend the mandate of existing futures until the new ones are fixed, it would be wise to review Article 101A in terms of reducing the consensus rate required in the institution of the Conference of Presidents from the existing 4/5 to 3/5 in order not to create any further malfunctions in the future. As far as Article 96(5) is concerned, both our rapporteur and the special rapporteur, Mr Davakis, have been covered. For articles containing transitional provisions, whose regulatory effect has long expired since 1975 or since 1967, it is clear that they must be abolished. Finally, as far as Articles 101 and 102 are concerned, we would say that the previous majority wanted to revise them, more appropriate to the fixed ideological positions on the left than because of serious pathogens that they may have. If we look carefully at the whole of Article 101 by the constitutional legislature of 1975 has made an excellent approach, complete and methodical, which describes the decentralised administrative, state system based on fundamental principles. This wise and general approach offers the joint legislator the basic basic choices that must be applied and take those measures which are in accordance with the changing historical, social and political conditions prevailing in the country. So I do not think that there is any - and there is no possibility - for supplementing, either for additions or for modifications, let us leave the provision as it is, to function. On the issue of local government agencies, Article 102 has also been wisely drafted by the constitutional legislator. The provision of Article 102 provides that the authorities and bodies of the local government shall be elected by secret and universal suffrage. Why should the analogous system be provided by the constitutional legislator? Let me remind you, ladies and gentlemen, that in the recent elections of local government, the electoral system implemented was one of the mere analogous, with the well-known deadlocks that it created and forced the current government to pass its first law on the rule of law for the municipalities. [[At this point the end of the speaking time of the honourable Member is ringing at the end of the bell.]] Therefore, after all these adventures, the New Democracy does not propose to revise paragraph 2 of this Article. Ladies and gentlemen, the achievement of a broad consensus, when next Monday we will be called upon to vote on the provisions of the constitutional reform, with the top of this minimum guaranteed income and the vote of the Greeks off-state, is a step towards upgrading our political culture. Solutions and facilities through the proposed regulatory content of the New Democracy Constitution will also upgrade our legal culture. Momentum is offered for this purpose and it imposes it. If he gets lost, he'll be lost for all of us and for all. Thank you very much.

As far as the issue of the Constitution is concerned, it is immediately noted that it is an agonizing attempt to maintain the bankrupt political system, which wants to legalise its criminal activities, invoking SYRIZA, once and for all, which betrayed both Greece and its ideology. This way should be based on the following five basic rules: firstly, a strong and stable executive power, secondly, an independent and strong parliament, numerically reflecting the precise ideological views of the citizens, thirdly, a truly independent and self-controlled justice, fourthly, a strong and independent local government and, fifthly, more importantly, the direct participation of citizens in all powers. As far as the first axis is concerned, if we hold a poll or a referendum, we will see that Greek citizens prefer presidential democracy. As far as the latter is concerned, the majority of Greeks are in favour of a mere analogy. As far as the third is concerned, literally everyone wants a real one and not just in the words independent and strong justice. As far as the fourth is concerned, which we shall be talking about next, everyone wants a strong and independent local government. Finally, as far as the fifth and most important, i.e. the participation of citizens in the governance of the country is concerned, it is a requirement of the entire Greek society, which now knows that this is the only way to recover Greece and never go bankrupt again. If all this is right and methodically started, Greece will not only recover its national sovereignty, independence, freedom and pride, it will not only return our children to their homeland, contributing to its reconstruction, but will evolve into the most beautiful, richer and happier country in the world, which is certainly due to our ancestors, who have inherited a unique civilization, but also to our children, who have condemned them to an unfinished end, because of our incredible irresponsibleness over the last 40 years. I now come to today's articles. 101A refers to the independent authorities. Pollution in the creation of independent authorities, which is a relatively recent institution, substantiates two versions: either that parliamentary democracy now faces problems in its operation and calls on others to resolve them or that these principles are imposed more generally and not only in Greece by the forces of globalisation, with a view to gradually dissipating and destroying the power of the national state. We believe that both are happening, on the basis of the kinds of independent authorities that are created, and the decisions they usually take against the basic characteristics of the national state. Recently, in Greece, a decision on the class of Religiouss in schools has been taken. It seems, therefore, clear that the characteristics of the decisions they take move in the logic of open society, that is to say, the logic of the free movement of capital, goods and people, of poor migrants. But that is exactly what globalisation is about to destroy the national state. Our view is that, instead of constantly creating independent authorities to resolve the malfunctions of the parliamentary system, it would be better to improve democratic institutions, particularly through the involvement of citizens in politics. In particular, as regards the revision of Article 101a, we have the following comments: Firstly, it comes for review with the strong majority of the two hundred and thirty Members, if I am not mistaken, which means that it is up to the exclusive will of the New Democracy to shape it as it wishes. Secondly, in paragraph 2 of the article, it is proposed that independent authorities should be established by a law that will vote a hundred and eighty Members. We necessarily agree with the idea of the proposal to be debated, but we propose adding a new paragraph, which will stipulate that, in order to create new independent authorities, in addition to what is provided for in the Constitution, the voting of two hundred Members will be required. Thirdly, as regards the appointment of the members of the authority, we propose that the paragraph 2 of the article should remain the same as the majority of the 4/5 of the Conference of Presidents. The Conference of Presidents should try to achieve the best possible composition of the members of an independent authority and not really be left to the individual majority. Fourthly, we agree on paragraph 3 to add a new subparagraph stating that the independent authorities are subject to the control of the House for their acts or omissions. As far as Article 102 is concerned, we propose the following: Firstly, we propose real decentralisation of the exercise of power and the establishment of the municipality and not just a formal bureaucratic decentralisation. Secondly, we say that there is a real presumption of competence in favour of local government. That is to say, whatever jurisdiction is not explicitly provided for in the Constitution's central government to belong to local government. Thirdly, we propose that the tax should be collected on local government. From the money it receives, it will retain the percentage allocated to it under law, while the rest will give it to the central government, so that the prudence of local government can be stopped by the government. Fourthly, the authorities of the local government bodies must be elected by universal and secret ballot by Greek citizens, with two separate ballots: one for the mayor or regional authority on a case-by-case basis, elected by an absolute majority, and one for the respective councils, for which their election will apply with the simple proportional system. If a candidate mayor or regional director respectively did not obtain an absolute majority, the election will be repeated next Sunday among the two relative majority. Fifthly, with a proposal from citizens representing 5% of the electorate, we propose that a binding local referendum should be held on any local issue. Sixthly, we propose that the present bodies of local government be publicly accounted for, before a public assembly, and that they lose their office in the event of a criminal conviction for offences that will provide for a relevant law on accountability. Seventhly, we propose any asset within the territory of a Local Government Agency - other than those of the national defence, national energy, telecommunications, national roads and railways - to be owned and managed by the respective Local Government. In conclusion, we are obviously in favour of the constitutional introduction of the proportional system to the elections for local government. We are also in favour of local referendums on the citizens' initiative, but these should be binding, both on the proposal for them and on the result. [[At this point the end of the speaking time of the honourable Member is ringing at the end of the bell.]] I need a minute, Mr President. Sadly, however, the SYRIZA law on local government has so many preconditions for holding the referendums that it will in practice renounce them. Finally, we are in favour of establishing local popular assemblies, but with decisive responsibilities, according to the Swiss standards. On the other hand, we disagree with the vote by foreigners on local government, considering that only those Greek citizens who are registered on the relevant electoral lists should vote. The information has already been released that the foreign registered on our country's electoral lists is two hundred and twenty thousand. Let us not confuse democracy here with the alteration of the nation. That would be extremely dangerous. However, our precise proposals for all the articles have been thoroughly analysed by our rapporteur and tabled, so we do not need to repeat them. Thank you very much.

According to this, the authorisation to prosecute shall be granted by Parliament if the request of the public prosecutor relates to an offence which is not linked to the performance of the duties or to the political activity of the Member. In giving the House the opportunity to give its opinion on the request for prosecution and to decide whether or not its content is linked to the action of the person as a Member, we are responding to the imperative dilemma that is raised between the freedom to perform the MP's duties and equality towards Greek citizens. At the same time, the three-month margin, so that the House is responsible for its President's decision, is the necessary safeguard clause for both the uninterrupted opening of parliamentary procedures and the unity of the Greek Parliament as a Parliament. The second indent is set out in Article 54 and the addition of paragraph 4 concerning the exercise of a electoral right at their place of residence of voters outside the territory. On the occasion of this arrangement, the opposition to the facilitation of foreign residents to vote from their homes as if they were second-class citizens. But it's not like that. On the contrary, it is about recognising the obvious obstacles for thousands of Greek citizens to exercise their constitutional right. It concerns the lifting of an injustice by the Greek state to its citizens who were forced during the crisis to change their place of residence. After all, participation in the leading expression of democracy is not a process that can be put into the balance of the costs of an air ticket from the place of residence to the pollen. SYRIZA's special speaker linked the right to vote outside the territory of Greek citizens to the risk of appreciable influence of the election result. It is, of course, a reasonable leap, since those citizens already have the right to vote and the regulation is about being able to exercise it. But let us bring this argument to its real dimension. Who can convincingly claim that my compatriot, who cannot come to his island to vote, if we give him the opportunity to do so, will alter the election result? The meaning of the intervention is much more important and the substance of the constitutional reform is deeper. It is the need for the Kefalonite and the Thios of the dispersal to have a say in whose country he is a citizen. We need to keep the flame that connects our place to his children who were forced to leave. It is the need for the voice of our citizens on the ground to be heard by institutions. Ladies and gentlemen, these two revised provisions may seem to be inconsistent. It may be reasonable to think that the right of Greek citizens to prosecute Members on the one hand and the electoral right of Greek citizens abroad on the other hand are matters which, at first reading, do not find a common point of incision. But is that really so? The answer lies in the invisible thread between social justice, freedom and the rule of law. What's social justice? It is a sense of citizens that their elected representatives are not above the law, that it is the citizen's status that determines our rights and obligations, while remaining unaffected by opportunities that parliamentary status can accompany. It is, finally, certain that the Member of Parliament who commits an offence will not be unpunished. On the other hand, social justice is the Greek citizen not to face any obstacles to the exercise of his constitutional rights. To force immigration to find work not to be a brake on its obligation to vote for the country of which it is a citizen. After all, is it social law to form two categories of citizens on the basis of their access to the electorate? What's freedom? It is the ability of the Member to legislate in his conscience in the way his opinion dictates. To be prosecuted at the request of the Public Prosecutor for acts which are unlawful and not those relating to the development of its policy. He can say his view fearlessly knowing that the House of Greeks can decide whether or not an act is unlawful. Freedom, too, is the citizen who has been forced or chose to build his future outside Greece to be able to vote for the future of himself and his homeland, to have the power to shape the political, economic and social conditions that will allow him to come back. And what is, finally, the rule of law? But, of course, ensuring that we are all accountable, according to the law, and he is fair, moral and easily accessible. It is the choice to agree that we coexist because we disagree and disagree because we have the right and the freedom to do so. This Constitutional Review is the opportunity not to repeat the vulnerability of the past. It is the occasion to rethink the way in which we want to organise our constitutional function. It is the solution to the dangers of the time to bring back to Greek citizens the sense of social justice, the sense of freedom and the certainty of the rule of law. Let's not lose it again. Thank you.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-22-S1-commons.u194	Thank you, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, the revision of the Constitution is an extremely important process in the life of a state. We would expect, however, that this key moment would be characterized by a review more bold, with greater broadness and richer vision, as we now have repressed - supposedly - the imperfections, shortcomings and mistakes that have been inherited from the period of post-population, but also from the experience of the crisis we are experiencing. These areas have particularly spatial, demographic and socio-economic characteristics, due to their distance and difficult access from and from the centre and urban centres in general, to demographic ageing and to their accelerated desertification. Thus, they are not always easily addressed to general government policies and they need special treatment, first to survive and stay alive. Only by reducing or eliminating regional and intra-regional disparities can they achieve their full economic and social development, since these regions also have comparative advantages to be created and to be at the forefront of any development strategy. In this context, the proposal to adapt the administrative model to the particular environment of these areas is also in progress, in order to ensure the proper and uninterrupted functioning of public administration in mountain and island areas and to adapt it to the specific features and circumstances there. The term local government shall comprise two parts clearly defined in Article 102. Locality and self-government. The above elements include the fundamental framework on which the organisation and functioning of local government bodies are built by the public and regulatory legislator. The questions raised are two: First, can the legislator, following the establishment of the Local Government bodies on the basis of the abovementioned components, intervene in their organisation and relocate to the basis of a different shape? In any event, is it subject to commitments entered into under the constitutional guarantee of self-government? And, secondly, are there limits to the power of the legislator to define the content of the administrative and financial autonomy of the local government bodies in its individual aspects that do not belong to their constitutionally regulated core? We, as a New Democracy, support the fact that the framework exists. What we can do is encourage the joint legislator to transfer more powers to local government. I was a municipal councillor in the Municipality of Thessaloniki and I know very well, I have experienced it, that the local government or the self-government has, as it deserves, neither financial self-interest, so that it cannot stand next to the citizen as defined in constitutional terms or as it should be. As a Member of Parliament, therefore, in my role and responsibilities, it is my duty to refuse to try to encourage the joint legislator to give those powers to local government, to strengthen both self-government and economic self-evidentness and perhaps tax decentralisation, always with strong guarantees of transparency. Fortunately, the current government has come, and by law it has tried to set appropriate balances, the appropriate conditions for municipalities and regions to function. In conclusion, I would like to say that I am associated with the proposals of the New Democracy and again stress my special debt at this historic parliamentary moment.

However, we need a more coordinated and integrated approach aimed at staying, through the improvement of the quality of life, people and better economic opportunities for those who want to allow their villages. In addition to investment and aid, there is also a need for such intervention and so much to serve social cohesion, i.e. it is possible for rural people to compare their level of life both in terms of income and living conditions to those of urban residents. And in this parameter, the main tools are those which can be served but also ensure adequate and complementary income. These areas are the agri-foodstuffs and the agri-tourism sector. The rural areas can, therefore, contribute to reversing or reducing the decline in their populations by exploiting these possibilities. But they need help. Above all, the European Commission needs to propose a strategy for the demographic problem, which gives priority to these regions and specific activities, in order to shape those conditions which will facilitate the satisfactory balance of professional and personal life to those who want to stay in their roots. There must also be an increase in the effectiveness of European and national funds allocated to mountain and island regions in order to improve their effectiveness and to serve cohesion, which is also one of the major problems in rural areas, which, at the same time, also means addressing the demographic problem. If these proposals are linked to a constitutional check, such as our own interpretative statement, which we propose, we will only then properly serve the existing constitutional requirement of Article 101(4), which says that the joint legislator and the administration when acting regulatoryly are obliged to take into account the particular circumstances of island and mountain areas by ensuring that they are developed. Ladies and gentlemen, after the end of the economic crisis and the memoranda, I would also like to focus on an issue relating to the need to draw conclusions so overall for the functioning of the European Union and our country, and I would particularly like to say to you on cases relating to the way in which public investment is distributed in our country, which, as a general rule, has been carried out so far in customer relations and the empirical diagnosis of needs. These procedures have made a decisively negative contribution to the emergence of the economic crisis. This must be changed by shifting the centre of gravity from a customer framework to a system of distribution of public investment on the basis of the needs of the economy and society. Here I would remind you that there was primarily the American example, and then the European experience followed, and in the 1980s, the independent authorities were also created in Greece. If I am not mistaken, in 1988 we have the first independent form, the local radio committee, and then many such bodies are set up which are summarised, I would say, in four categories. First, principles that act as institutional guarantees, second, principles which exercise supervision over public administration, third, market surveillance principles and fourth, principles of a mixed nature guaranteeing fundamental rights, but at the same time supervising the functioning of the market. However, here I would remind you that following the Review of the 2001 Constitution, the Personal Data Protection Authority, the National Broadcasting Council, the Communications Security Authority, the Supreme Personnel Selection Council and, of course, the Citizens' Counselor are incorporated into it. It is, ladies and gentlemen, a top moment, because only their reference to the Constitution surrounds them with particular prestige and automatically, I would say, gain further influence and power, and they take away powers from the executive, putting the question of parliamentary control into question. In view of the fact that, firstly, they are placed out of the administrative hierarchy and are excluded from the hierarchical and administrative control of the feasibility and legality of their actions, and secondly, they are formed by members who can guarantee personal and political independence, we propose that it be clearly added that the plenary should exercise direct control over the administrations of the independent authorities, because today we see this paradox as paradox, the Minister should enjoy parliamentary control. On the subject of control, our proposal here is to aim at ensuring the public political control over the functioning of these five independent authorities, which are now also included in the Constitution. We also think that restrictions should be introduced to create new independent authorities, because today we have a total of thirty independent authorities. As our rapporteur rightly pointed out, excessive recourse to the institution of the independent authorities poses problems, I would say, of democracy, because it reveals a fear on the part of the executive to carry out its duties. With this reasoning we propose that the creation of an independent authority should be abolished, but also that it should be changed, that it should be required to require a majority of 3/5 of the House. We also propose the constitutional recognition of the Competition Committee and the Securities and Market Committee.
