ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-22-S1-commons.u218	Thank you, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, for reasons of historical symbolism, I too welcome the proposal that the renumbering of the Constitution should end up in Article 114. Otherwise, I choose to focus on Articles 101A and 102, not only because I have had the honour of being in charge of the independent authority and then carried out two terms as mayor, but because I am deeply convinced that strong independent authorities and a strong local government are upgrading the democratic city. I will try to explain why, starting with the independent authorities. From the payment of the independent authorities, there is a horrifying automation in this Chamber at times. Politicians from all the wings of the House are resenting the political system, because it constantly appeals to the creation of independent authorities. I am not saying that they are completely wrong, but this selfishness, let me say, also contains a dose of hypocrisy. Let us see, ladies and gentlemen, the independent authorities which have constitutional assurances. Each of them fulfils a mission requiring guarantees of objectivity and impartiality, which in fact does not have the traditional executive power in charge of the Government. That is why our Constitution takes away from the government majority and assigns it to the enhanced majority of the Conference of Presidents. The enhanced majority is inevitably linked to the validity of these principles. On the subject of the prestige of certain institutions, I referred in detail the day before yesterday, on the occasion of the institution of the President of the Republic. I have tried to explain that it is absolutely incompatible with the substance of the institution of the President of the Republic's institution to show the institution of office by a majority. The Constitution, as it stands today, resorts to the relevant majority, since it has now exhausted the last solution to the elections. But that is exactly what we need to avoid now. It is not only necessary, therefore, but especially an integral part of the authority to elect it from the widest possible majority in Parliament, because that is the only way the President of the Republic can act as a regulator of the constitution. Similarly, the Constitution rightly provides for an enhanced majority in favour of the appointment of the five constitutionally guaranteed independent authorities, since, as I said, the mission of each of them requires that it be obtained by a qualified majority, which exceeds the one that supports the government. In particular, the Privacy Authority, as well as the Communications Security Authority, are responsible for the protection of individual rights which, as specific private life manifestations, are threatened by the mechanisms of executive authority. The National Broadcasting Council is responsible for overseeing the proper implementation of the guarantees provided for in Article 15 of the Constitution, thanks to pluralism and pluralism resulting from freedom of expression and information. At the same time, these freedoms are fundamental guarantees of the democratic culture. That is true, therefore, and this is where supervision of the application of the corresponding guarantees is entrusted to an independent body. The same must be said of the Citizens' Counsel, as this is a principle of mediation between public administration and private individuals, but it is also a protector of constitutional rights. The APRC is the only authority that responds to the need to respond to a chronic suffering of the political system, to the incompetence of governments, regardless of party colour, to respect democracy in the service of public administration. In short, in the clientele appointments to the public. However, the PCA, ladies and gentlemen, has saved us. At a time when I was a mayor, when unemployment had reached unthinkable heights, we claimed, myself and other mayors, unbearable psychological pressures on appointments. ASAPA was our shield. And not to forget, it is a creation of PASOK and this, as is the other three of the four independent principles I have mentioned. Of course, at times the joint legislator has also created independent principles that should normally not exist, such as the Public Revenue Authority. But here, we should do what we did with the SAPA. Instead of meditating, we can support the beginning. If one day it turns out that the Greek governments are capable of collecting taxes without resorting to any kind of customer facilities, then let us abolish the principle. But until then, let us not exaggerate with parliamentary control. I do not support, of course, that the independent authorities should be unregulated. Each one of them exercises power, and in a democracy there cannot be power that is not accountable. We must, however, find a way of monitoring which balances between independence and the necessary accountability. If we look at Parliament's Rules of Procedure, we will see that it ensures adequate control over the independent authorities. Check first of all, through the reports submitted and may also be carried out by the committee responsible, and not only by the Committee on Institutions and Transparency. However, there is also an extraordinary moment of control over the independent authorities, which is enshrined in Rule 14 of the Rules of Procedure of the House, in which the powers of the Conference of Presidents are regulated. Paragraph (g) of Article 14 provides that the Conference of Presidents shall, where necessary, invite any independent authority on matters relating to the performance of its tasks under the Constitution. This, ladies and gentlemen, is a form of control which is extremely active. That is why the legislator has chosen control by the Conference of Presidents, whose meetings are secret. It is wise to choose the legislator. It would like to see effective control by the independent authority, which is called upon to answer to the institution which has indicated it, which is the Conference of Presidents. Active control but discreet control away from publicity lights. Discrimination is imposed by the very nature of the institution, which is incompatible with normal parliamentary scrutiny carried out either on the Government collectively or individually on its members. Because in the case of the government the principle of government solidarity is being activated. Every minister under parliamentary control has, in general, almost always the support of his colleagues and the Prime Minister, as well as the political support of the political party to which he belongs. This does not apply in the case of the independent authorities. The President of an independent authority at the time he comes to the House is unable to count by definition on the support of anyone, precisely because he is independent. He's completely alone. On the basis of all the things I have developed, it is therefore regrettable that, rather than making sure that we broaden the basis of the independent authorities, we shrink it down. Look at the composition of the Conference of Presidents today. The Conference of Presidents shall have twenty-three members. Of these twenty-three members are members of the parliamentary majority. And we are reducing the four-fifths that are needed, in accordance with the current Constitution, to bring the members of the independent authorities together and get it to just 3/5 of the Conference of Presidents. Do you know what this means practically today? That it is enough for the twelve members of the parliamentary majority plus two members of either of the other parties, of any party, since all have a minimum of two members This does not give rise to a broad political basis to the independent authorities. It shrinks them. In the field of local government, I dare say that the Constitution already leaves us with enormous scope for strengthening the institution, since it complements the principles of proximity and subsidiarity enshrined in the European Charter of Local Government of the Council of Europe, which our country has ratified, and in this case has increased formal power. However, important steps were taken with the Constitutional Review of 2001. The new constitutional provisions of Article 102 introduced the presumption of competence for local affairs, authorised the legislator to systematically define the scope and their categories, confer powers on local government which constitute a state's mission, and, in addition to the immediate application of the clause for the transfer of corresponding resources - whenever it is transferred to local government, it is also possible to provide for local revenue that can be specified, defined and collected by local government bodies themselves. If, indeed, one reads carefully Decision 1181/2014 of the Council of State, one will understand that that court means taxes and not just revenues. When, ladies and gentlemen, the day comes when a municipality itself can tax citizens, then we can say that we have finally come to the ageing of local self-government. I conclude, ladies and gentlemen, by saying that I am personally in favour of SYRIZA's proposal for constitutional ratification in local elections and local referendums for foreigners who have completed five years of continuous and legal residence in the country. The reason for this is obvious to me. We cannot talk about the essential integration of these foreigners into the Greek society unless they enjoy all the rights they complete at the level of the Constitution, the human person. Individual and social rights alone, therefore, are not enough. They also need the rights of political participation, because they act as a guarantee for the other two. Ladies and gentlemen, the debate on the vote of the non-stated Greeks, with emphasis on the need to maintain a relationship with the mother - home, it is not fair that this institution should be ignored for foreigners legally resident in the country, who work in and raise their children here. Thank you.

This refers to the obligation of the joint legislator and administration to take account of the particular circumstances of island and mountain areas in our country, while ensuring their development. We therefore believe that the general and vague reference to development has, from the experience of at least to date, shown that, with the exception of minimum exceptions, this explicit provision of the Constitution for the island and mountain regions has been left empty, in practice it has not worked, so we have proposed, on the one hand, that it be added to paragraph 4 that it is for the whole social and economic development of the island regions and, on the other hand, for Article 101 we have proposed an interpretative addition, which recognises the need to adapt the administrative model to the particular environment of island and mountain regions, by drawing up specific operational plans for them and by providing incentives for civil servants who are serving in them. This comes to complement an overall policy, which we have done over the last four years, in relation to the remote, island regions, the mountain regions of our country, which have operated as a poor relative for the political system over the past decades. The law defines the establishment and operation of local folk assemblies, as well as the conditions and manner of holding local referendums and local initiatives at local level. Foreigners, who have completed five years of continuous and legal residence in the country, have the right to vote and to stand in local elections and participate in local referendums, as laid down in law. I think, however, that for the Greek society this 'as it is' cannot be the message. The reason is simple: the economic crisis and its consequences do not allow us to return to 1986 or 2001. On the contrary, they force us to incorporate the experience of the crisis and to see how the statute of our constitution will reflect the intention and will of the state for a social contract. That will shield democracy and inspire confidence in the citizens. ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-22-S1-commons.u235	The speaker of the New Democracy Mr George Stylos has the floor.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-22-S1-commons.u236	Thank you very much, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, today's debate on the constitutional review, for us younger Members, is a very special debate. It is a debate that goes beyond the perpetual political conflicts. It is about the future of the country, its history, that enables all of us to overcome ourselves. So I would like to start with some basic assumptions on my part. The request for a radical revision of the Constitution is not now. Each crisis brings to the surface the paths of the past, it damages the political system as a whole, it reduces citizens' confidence, it damages politicians and state institutions. Result? The change in the charter of the country is a panacea. But let us be honest with our citizens. A country's constitution cannot in itself solve the paths of state, economic and social reality. As in any regulatory text, it defines the framework of political life and provides the tools for the responsible state actors, and so we have to deal with it. This does not mean that no changes should be made. After all, many years have passed since the last extensive constitutional review of 2001, with the effort made in 2008. The ever-changing reality, however, goes beyond, in many cases, even the letter of the Constitution. Therefore, we would be blind, if we did not acknowledge the problematic nature of certain provisions, which we are now coming to review, but taking into account three main assumptions: Firstly, the 1975 Constitution proved to be a successful constitution, on which the most stable and longest-lived democracy in our country was founded. Secondly, the importance of the constitutional text cannot be called into question, but it is constantly falling under conditions of European integration and internationalization. I am referring to institutions such as the European Court of Human Rights, the Court of Justice of the European Union, which are now two main pillars of the protection of fundamental rights in the European Union. Thirdly, there is the informal and lasting constitutional change, notably through the case-law of the courts, which adapts the Constitution to the changing circumstances at hand. Ladies and gentlemen, it is worth noting that this is where we arrived after a serious job and an overreach. It is, I think, a good thing that today's constitutional review comes from a parliament, where the majority were another ideological direction before the elections and now we have a majority of different ideological directions. We could - I think - have agreed on more revised articles by Parliament, but I would like to keep the positive from the whole process. The Review Committee has sometimes come up with simple and sometimes increased majority voting to seven thematic changes proposed. I'll mention the most basics in my hierarchy. Firstly, the introduction of a state obligation for a minimum guaranteed income system to support the Greek family, I am referring to Article 21. I am referring to the Greek family, which was the strong cell, which lasted during the economic and social crisis of the last ten years and kept the Greek society upright, a liberal measure which, unfortunately, was not the courage of the previous government to vote for it. On the initiative of the New Democracy, the principle of a social market economy, which, as a condition, has already been promoted, is enshrined in the fundamental European treaties on the initiative of the European People's Party. Second positive provision: the abolition of the deadline laid down in Article 86 relating to criminal offences of Ministers in the performance of their duties and the equilibrium with those applicable to all citizens. Thirdly, restricting parliamentary immunity, Rule 62, only in acts or declarations relating to the performance of the duties of the Member. I believe in these changes that they shield the credibility of our political system, which we so much need today. Fourthly, the complete release of the procedure for the election of the President of the Republic from parliamentary elections, Rule 32, so that the democratic people's mandate for a government is not interrupted and that the procedure for the election of the highest authority from the opposition in question is not used as a tool. I think it is a pity to mention the positive effects of a full four-year government cycle, where citizens will ultimately evaluate the work done. Fifthly, facilitating the vote of Greeks abroad, Article 54, in such a way that it does not address issues of unconstitutionality, as decided by the Intergroup. I believe that we have made our minimum debt to the Greeks abroad, with the right to vote, but also to the generation of Brain Drain. Obviously, we could have taken more steps forward. Unfortunately, however, the previous House, which was the nominee, severely reduced the scope of the revised articles. Due to SYRIZA’s ideas, the provisions relating to Article 24 on the protection of the environment, climate change and cultural heritage have remained out of procedure, as well as the inclusion in the Constitution of provisions concerning financial rules. We are all aware of the positive effects, which would have a stable tax system, which cannot be changed by a government and could exceed the four-year cycle. Finally, regarding the establishment of non-state universities, private universities: in particular the revision of Article 16, today's Customisation kept it captive to the state monopoly, for a whole generation, preventing the country from moving into an international training centre which would lead to the return of thousands of scientists and would allow us to import, instead of exporting students, currency and jobs. However, I would like to keep the positive from the whole process, that we all agree, even in a few provisions. Let this be the consensus that we have shown, a guide to follow-up, for the major reforms that the country so much needs and so much the need for Greek citizens. Thank you very much.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-22-S1-commons.u237	Thank you, too. Mr Ioannis Pascalides from New Democracy has the floor.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-22-S1-commons.u238	Thank you, Mr President. Mr Ministers, ladies and gentlemen, with the present amendments and the abolition of individual articles, we are seeking to modernise our constitution, which has been judged to complement the current data, given that the Constitution does not only regulate the establishment and exercise of state power, regulates much more, regulates the relationship between the state and society, the relationship between the state and the economy and the relationship between the state and the European Union and the international community. Indeed, the country needs a brave Revision of the Constitution, especially if one takes into account that in Germany the 1949 Constitution has been amended more than a hundred times in France the 1958 Constitution more than thirty times. This Review House, with the amendment of paragraph 5 of Article 96 of the Constitution, comes to correct a serious injustice against military judges in relation to other judges. The judges of the Armed Forces, those judging the cases of thousands of children serving the country, looked so many years after. The state may have passed a law, implemented the constitutional order with a delay, of course, twenty years and given military justice all those guarantees so that the judges are independent and life-long, in two words they are no different from the other judges, but they have not completed the constitutional adjustment exercise. They argued - and they were quite right - that 'we cannot be judges in all but wages', saying, moreover, that they give justice to thousands of soldiers, they handle difficult cases, some of which are extremely important, they are not related to the Armed Forces, they are traded and their service changes are made by the Supreme Judicial Council, while the judgments of the military courts are set out in the Marios Ice. However, their most important argument is the provision of the Constitution which led the state to establish a special salary for judges, that is to say, the provision that judges should be paid according to their function, because the special salary for judges is an institutional guarantee to ensure their operational and personal independence, without the implementation of which the joint legislator has just declared themselves in the country's constitutional charter. It is this injustice that is being lifted by the equalisation of military judges with the other judges, which is brought about by the revision of Article 96(5), by asserting the guarantees of the independent functioning of military justice and by removing them from the outdated logic of the courts. After all, as Mr Javarra has said, this independence and institutional equality with other judges are not for the sake of their industry, but for the sake of the citizens, who are not allowed, according to the Constitution, to be tried by judges with limited guarantees of independence. The independent authorities are collectively administrative bodies, integrated into the legal person of the State, i.e. the State, which appeared in the Greek legal system as early as the 1980s as independent administrative authorities and some of them were subsequently guaranteed by the Constitution itself, by the 2001 Review, which regulates the selection procedure of their members, sets out their basic rules of operation and defines their responsibilities, and the extension of the independence of those structures was removed from the constitutional text. The vast majority of the independent authorities are, however, provided for by the common legislation, and often their establishment is a fulfillment of the Greek Republic's obligation as a Member State of the European Union. However, over the last few years, it has been seen over-the-board by these authorities, which reflects the impossibility of the political system to assume their responsibilities and tries to move them to the shoulders of non-political persons, as Mr Stylianides has rightly pointed out, and it is not too often that, because of the current provision of Rule 101(2) for an increased four-fifths majority, as regards the selection of the members of the administration by the Conference of Presidents of Parliament, they have remained in their place for a very long time, even though their term of office has expired. In this introductory interpretative declaration, which has a broad consensus, the above percentage is changed from four fifths to three fifths in order to avoid problems in the future and to ensure the continuous and undisturbed functioning of the independent authorities. Local government, as has been structured in our country, but also internationally, is one of the institutions of political participation and already with the previous Review, the competences are shielded twice: firstly, the introduction of the presumption of competence for local affairs for OTA, thus limiting the law to allocating local affairs according to their scope to Agencies A' and B' degrees B' and, secondly, to the drastic strengthening of the administrative and financial autonomy of OTAs. The right to local government is a political right and the activities of local government, the development of the principles of local government and the functioning of local government is a very substantial and practical method of exercising civil rights, which is guaranteed by universal and secret vote. In fact, it could be said that this function of local government goes beyond the local responsibilities of its institutions, because very often local government also operates as an institution of political pressure and not as an institution of competence. In the light of the foregoing, it is therefore demonstrated that existing provisions of the Constitution, which concern local authorities, provide for the proper organisation and functioning of them, and in particular Article 102(2) thereof, contains all democratic guarantees of the choice of authorities and does not require any modifications. To sum up, I would like to stress that the revision of the Constitution is not a matter for the government, but for all Parliament. It is at the top of our democratic culture, and that is why we must all stand up to the level of the situation with responsibility and harmony. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-22-S1-commons.u242	Mr President, I do not know if it is customary for me, but my position is entitled 'The Wind Revision'. Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, I have heard many speakers express the view that the ongoing constitutional review is a lost opportunity, with a view to limiting paragraph 6 of Article 110 of the Constitution to take a new revision initiative. However, whoever shares the definition of Bismarck for politics, I think he will agree that the dimension of this Constitutional Review really moves to the edge of the practicable, even if not the desirable. Given the conditions and the divisive climate, which had been created in previous years, and the changes to important articles of the Constitution that are likely to arise, I would say that from the ongoing process we are getting the best possible result. For the truth, let each one of us recall in his memory the dramatic events of the previous period, in which the pregnant Review was fertilized, in order to understand its importance. The country was exhausted from the Multiannual Economic Crisis. Society disoriented sought lost ideals and values. The forces of nationalism dominated political life. The state functions were constantly questioned. Greece was one step ahead of the collapse. In those circumstances, could one well expect the consensus needed to achieve significant constitutional changes in critical and major issues? In this toxic environment, however, some political forces have managed to break to the level of circumstances. The New Democracy's party has demonstrated tolerance and demonstrated its deep relationship with society. He spoke to the citizens the language of truth and took the responsibility to definitively remove the country from the political and economic stagnation. Finally, in these circumstances, the sober and responsible political attitude of today's majority manages to turn the lost opportunity into a won Review. Allow me, ladies and gentlemen, to say that the constitutional changes, which were born in the ashes of the crisis, reflect the destiny of this nation to record successes, even when everything seems in vain and impossible. In this top democratic process every political area assumes its responsibilities for the changes made to our charter, but also to those reserved for other legislators in the constitutional path of the country. As a Member of the New Democracy, however, I will have the honour to say that in this Review House I was a member of the parliamentary majority, who put its seal on the fourth amendment of the 1975 Constitution. I shall be proud to say that I voted in favour of the revision of paragraph 1 of Article 21 on the introduction of a minimum guaranteed income system and perhaps a top change, concerning the revision of paragraph 4 of Article 32 and the disconnection of the President of the Republic from the dissolution of the House. The fact is that in the past, unfortunately, and recently, the forecasts for the election of the President of the Republic had been misused in order to serve party purposes. But the conditions have now matured both in society and in the political world to disconnect this process from the exhaustion of government service. Sadly, however, on this issue, which has also dealt with previous reform procedures and has been a constant and widespread demand from the political world and society, the Responsibility of Civic Response has been the cause of stumbling up, with an unprecedented and sudden objection of unconstitutionality on the ground of unsolved legal issues, which has also been a pretext and an absolute one. I will, however, also have the opportunity to vote for the fourth paragraph of Article 54 to facilitate Greeks living outside the Greek territory in the exercise of their electoral right, a matter which has been a strong concern for political dialogue and which has been a commitment of the Prime Minister over the past period. Thus, ensuring the equitable exercise of the electoral right to Greek foreigners will constitute an emblematic constitutional intervention, which will be signed by Mr.Mitsotakis. I will, however, vote for the revision of Rule 62, according to which Parliament's permission to prosecute a Member of Parliament against a Member is necessarily granted when the request from the public prosecutor relates to a offence which is not linked to the exercise of the duties or to the political activity of the Member. The relevant regulation achieves a balance between the protection of the Members' freedoms in his political action and the restriction of immunity only in acts referred to therein. In addition, I will be voting in favour of the revision of paragraph 2 of Article 68 to allow parliamentary minorities to request a committee of inquiry on a proposal from at least 10 Members. In this way, this process regains its true character, which is the exercise of parliamentary control by the parties. This is undoubtedly a democratic regulation that promotes parliamentary operation. I will also be voting in favour of the very important addition of the sixth paragraph to Article 73 of the Constitution, which establishes the right to legislative initiative for citizens by signing a motion for a law by five hundred thousand voters, which strengthens democratic processes and strengthens citizens' participation in political events. Furthermore, I will have the opportunity to vote in favour of the revision of Rule 86(3) by removing the paragraph which restricted Parliament's competence to prosecute against members of the government or of the State Secretarys only until the end of the Second Regular Session of the Parliamentary Period. Indeed, this provision has for many years been a reason to question the transparency and integrity of political life and its abolition serves to restore the political dignity and the recovery of society's trust from the political world. Unfortunately, in this case too, the proposal by the Minister of Staff Response, which, in interpretative terms, is supposedly retrospective, in order to distinguish the offences committed in the exercise of the minister's duties and not on them, is motivated by communications springs, since in any event it cannot be implemented, because it is contrary to the criminal basis. I will also vote in favour of the revision of Rule 96(5), which brings about the full assimilation of the military judges with the officials, as well as the revision of paragraph 2 of Rule 101a, which facilitates the selection of the persons administering the independent authorities, since the three-fifths of the Conference of Presidents of the Parliament will now be required to ensure that they do not appear vacant in the filling of the seats and do their job without hindrance. In voting in favour of these proposals, ladies and gentlemen, I will have the right to say that I participated in the 2019 Wind Revision as a member of the only party involved in the resolution of the 1975 Constitution and its subsequent Reviews, which demonstrates the conscientiousness that this party has in the consciousness of the citizens of the country. To conclude, Mr President, I would like to congratulate the President of the Commission, Mr Stylianidis, on the work of the two review bodies on their hard and systematic efforts and to wish our new Constitution a peaceful and creative path for the country. Thank you very much.

It did not present a comprehensive plan for the operation of the general government bodies pyramid. As far as local government is concerned, instead of ensuring decentralisation by transferring responsibilities and resources, it has broken with fragmentary and targeted provisions even on its own. It has committed violently to the responsibilities of municipal regional councils and their functioning in the name of supposedly governmentality. The constitutional framework therefore needs to be strengthened so that the joint legislator is forced to ensure both decentralisation and self-interest. Our proposal in Article 101(1) is in this direction by explicitly providing that the regional organisation system of the state will be organised by law, a system of regional organisation that will not shrink the OTAs into fiscal mechanisms, as the government is now trying to do, but will provide them with the means of realising their local policies. Because the municipality cannot make economic, development and any other planning when the central government expects the distribution of financial resources when waiting for the CFP. In Europe more than eight hundred municipalities have been building participatory budgets for two decades. On the contrary, in our country, municipalities draw up budgets without even knowing how much money they will be managed every year. Although they have competence under the Constitution for the responsibility of local things and affairs, they often lack the ability to respond to this responsibility. We are proposing the establishment of appropriate operational management plans and the provision of incentives for civil servants serving in these areas. Finally, ladies and gentlemen, I want to refer to Article 102(2). Our proposal is to introduce a direct vote with a proportional system to highlight the principles of local government. I will focus on the most important, in particular, the introduction of a proportional system to highlight the principles of local government. The introduction of simple proportionally ensures democratic deepening in the constitutional requirements for equal voting, the service of the representative authority, the participation and involvement of local society in self-government. Are we really thinking, ladies and gentlemen, that simply proportional is a system that functionally strengthens decentralisation? Because it strengthens social dissemination in local affairs by broadening citizens' participation and the obligation to convergences and compositions. Thus, administrative and economic decentralisation, i.e. transferring responsibilities and resources to local government, is not limited to managing local things by an artificial majority, but is a matter for the whole of local society. Besides, European experience demonstrates that decentralised and successful self-government models go hand in hand with simple electoral systems proportional to most of Europe's municipalities. Unfortunately, New Democracy does not have the simple analogue in its DNA, and this explains the absence of investment in decentralisation. In reality, however, while this particular provision, the State in general and vaguely can take into account the particular circumstances of the islands and mountainous regions, has, as I said, set for the first time, at the very first stage protection of those areas that need so much attention and protection from the Greek state, almost empty letters have remained. With few exceptions, the Greek state did in fact act, legislated, drafted laws and, in particular, acts which really helped the islands, especially the small, and mountainous regions. You know that our country has a special environment, it has a geography, a biodiversity that differs from most European Union countries, so I think the Greek state should take this into account. What the House has actually done is this provision in the Constitution today, to try from an empty letter to make it a provision that has or at least will try to set terms in the state and have a effect on the daily lives of the citizens of our islands and the remote mountain areas. Why am I saying that? The interpretative addition proposed by the proposed parliament puts two basic things in place. What's the problem with the islands, actually? We may all have in mind the islands in the summer months, which may have tourism, we may feel like ideal places to live, but the reality of an islander is not that. The reality of January, February, December is completely different from the one the middle citizen may have in mind. The reality of these months is that it shakes the pamphlet of every island - because the islanders have lived in the last five years, for them I have been responsible for, I have been able to share this experience with them, and I can take you in here - it has the agony if it has a pediatrician in the multivalent doctorate to take the child of two years, three years old, if a fever that can go up - and may be a daily life very simple - can be fatal. I am not saying that things happen very often, but as mayor, I had to have people who trembled, enter the Ministries and say: 'Hey, a pediatrician we want, a pediatrician!'. Also, as mayor, I was trying - as a small municipality, not of the very small, there are worse - and I had the luck and the joy of returning too many islands at this time, because we created a network of islands, and to see the difficulties they faced, not having technical services. I sincerely speak to you, the Municipality of Kastelorizou has two million in its reserve at its fund. He can't do a project! I believe that this has nothing to do with political shade or visual left, right or centre. It is about dealing with daily problems. The European Union is the first time in 2016 that it received, even in a document, the concept of islandity, by a resolution of the European Parliament, and that was positive. We, of course, are mainly an island country. Of the three hundred and fifty-five - if I am not mistaken, there are - 30% of resident islands of the European Union. So the first ones we should have cared for, had we put this part on the table in the European institutions too. However, even in 2016, the European Parliament, in its resolution of 4 February, takes this seriously into account. Immigration - because this is the reality, which is why the European Parliament has taken into account - must understand that the islands of Greece and Italy in particular, but also of Spain, are faced with the largest part of the influx of very large refugee groups and then understand that there is a difference in islands. I would remind everyone that our islands, especially the smaller ones, have nothing to do with Sicily or Sardinia or Corsica. Not all the islands are like that. If you look at it and populations, Greece still has the greatest problem. So what do we suggest? Honestly, it is a wish, in view of the Monday's vote, to reconsider this issue. I don't want to blame or say anything else. But that is where the answer has been given by the act. We have voted on the transport balance, which I hope will continue on better terms every year. It was a constant request from the islanders, because we have very big problems. ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-22-S1-commons.u275	We're closing with Mrs. Olga Kefefaliannis from New Democracy.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-22-S1-commons.u276	Thank you very much, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, it has been more than a year since the debate in the House on the Constitutional Review. In a climate of polarisation, the then government of SYRIZA deliberately sought the disorientation of Greek society, with a vehicle for constitutional revision, in order to overshadow the enormous problems of economic bleeding, unemployment, the critical national issues, which were faced with incalculable costs for the country. Somehow, unfortunately, it was anticipated that the cubit of the proposed parliament would go very low. Of course, history will seek out the reasons why this top institutional process has once again been mutilated. As happened in 2008 - I was then a Member of Parliament in my first term - and now the voices of populism have cut Greek society off from progress and modernisation in critical areas of state function, education and the environment. However, it cannot and should not be judged to be the result of this Review, as well as its offer to the next generations. Whether by simple or increased majority, the Review Committee was converging at seven and very important points to be revised. One of these is the equation, in accordance with Article 96(5), of the military judges with the other judges. This is a proposal from the current parliamentary majority, which achieves access to all the constitutional privileges of judicial status, to restore the current deficit found in guaranteeing the personal and operational independence of the military judges. As we know, with the current Constitution, justice is granted by courts set up by ordinary judges, who enjoy functional and personal independence. Under this provision of Article 87(1) of the Constitution, the legislator - and rightly so - took care, within the then post-political climate, to dismantle the function and work of members of military justice from within the structures of the Armed Forces. Of course, in accordance with Article 96(5) of the Constitution, the courts, courts, naval courts and tribunals shall be constituted by a majority of members of the Judiciary of the Armed Forces and those members, as reasonably plausible, are surrounded by the guarantees of the operational and personal independence referred to in Article 87(1) of the Constitution. However, the plenary session of the Council of State in 2003 did not accept in its decision that military judges were entitled to the special salary of judges, on the grounds that Article 96 of the Constitution does not refer to Article 88(2) on the remuneration of judges. Ladies and gentlemen, the guarantees of the operational and personal independence of the military judges cannot be partially provided. Both the wage issue and all other issues must come from these basic guarantees. The indiscriminate alteration of the constitutional guarantees of the personal and operational independence of military justice contributes to the upgrading of the relationship between democracy and justice. Moreover, the term "military judge" does not reflect the military status of the judge but its functional role. Both symbolically and essentially does not mean in a democratic European state to maintain any discrimination in the constitutional guarantees provided by judicial officers. And through the common legislative procedure, after the entry into force of this revised provision, the way for more modern legal arrangements is opened, with respect for military justice, for the principles of the European Convention on Human Rights, according to which every citizen, in any capacity, is tried by an independent and impartial court. I am going to Article 101a of the Constitution, which concerns the establishment and functioning of a relatively modern type of administrative body in the European legal order, the independent authorities whose function is to protect the independence of administrative action against the rights of the rightholders, but also to ensure the impartiality of the executive from the influence of the political authority. As we all know, apart from the five constitutionally provided independent authorities, there are others provided for by the law. There is no doubt that the work carried out by the independent authorities is essential and their operation is governed by quality characteristics limited to the State administration. Their operation has substantially contributed to the protection of personal data, broadcasting, public administration and other critical sectors. It is precisely because the independent authorities are addressing sensitive issues about the exercise of power of the State to the citizen that there is a need for a substantial modernisation of their establishment and operation, because there are many cases where the political system itself has played an important role in their operation. It should be recalled that in the previous government the TV licence law turned the National Telecommunications and Post Commission into an administrative structure of the Ministry of Infrastructure, while the same law removed substantial powers from the ERR to give the then Minister of State even more power. We must also remind ourselves of the successive deadlocks at the time, which, in the case of the National Broadcasting Council, have hit the prestige of the process. So, in a substantial effort to protect the credibility of the institution, the parliamentary majority has proposed a revision of the relevant article. It seeks, in other words, to form possible consensus, by reducing the required consensus in the institution of the Conference of Presidents from four fifths to three fifths, as a solution to the established vulnerability of the Greek political system to take decisions of broad party acceptance. But finding common partners, beyond and above party reasonable, must always be a constant demand for modern European states in the states that operate on the basis of liberal democratic values. Ladies and gentlemen, both the core of the review proposal and the general political attitude of the current parliamentary majority, puts the historical need for our country to develop, modernise, deserved democracy and credibility at the highest institutional and political level. The axis of political normality and the balance of institutions is reflected at every opportunity in the initiatives taken by this government, with the aim of ensuring the continuity of the state in conditions of more effective democracy, where the economy recovers and hope returns, a democracy where the functions of the state are distinct, with clear responsibilities and extensive accountability for Greek citizens and future generations, in which we owe more than we have done so far. And because I am the last speaker on the part of Members, I would like to close with the hope that in the next Review of the Constitution we will be more bold, more shallow and more progressive. Thank you very much.
